
> /superpowers:using-superpowers Some context before we start. This repo is my
take-home for Tequity. The brief is Tequity-take-home-Outbox-relay.pdf in the
root. Read all of it first, and never edit it. CLAUDE.md has the rules we work
by.

The product is a small outbox relay. Tequity's load generator posts events to
our intake API on port 8080, up to 100 a second, and a few users send far more
than the rest. We store each event in Postgres before we answer. Workers then
pass each event on to their endpoint on port 9000, exactly once and in
created_at order per user. That endpoint returns 500s, rate limits with 429 and
Retry-After, answers slowly or goes down, and it saves duplicates if it gets
the same event twice. To grade it they clone the repo onto a clean Linux box
and run docker compose up, nothing else. They also go through the full git
history and this chat, so both need to show how we reached each decision.

So far the repo has only the PDF and CLAUDE.md, in two commits. No code and no
design yet.

First job: write the problem statement to docs/problem.md so every later
session starts from it. Use ecc:product-capability for this. Cover what we are
building and who calls it. Cover what the brief fixes: ports, the payload, what
each answer from 9000 means, the four pass conditions, how they run it and
what we hand in. Then what must always hold, what is out of scope, and the open
points the brief leaves to us. For each open point, add one line on which pass
condition it can break.

Open points I already know of: localhost inside a container is the container
itself, events can reach us out of created_at order, one request in flight per
user caps how fast a heavy user can go, a long outage leaves a backlog that has
to clear within the 5 minute window, jsonb changes the body so we may need the
raw bytes, the same event_id can arrive with a different body, 9000 can return
status codes that are not in their table, we can crash in the middle of a
send, and Retry-After may pause everything or only one user. Add anything I
missed.

Problem only. No solutions or design choices, those come next, one at a time.
Prose, plain words, around 800 words. Add one line to CLAUDE.md telling every
session to read docs/problem.md first. One commit for both. Use anti-ai for the
doc and the commit message, and bullets in your chat reply.

● I wrote the problem statement to docs/problem.md, added the read-it-first line
  to CLAUDE.md, and committed both together as 27b73a5 ("Write down the problem
  the brief sets, before any design").

  What the doc covers

  - What we build and who calls it. Tequity's load generator calls the intake
    API on port 8080. Our workers call Tequity's endpoint on port 9000. Nobody
    else uses it.
  - What the brief fixes:
    - the ports and the example payload
    - what each answer from port 9000 means
    - how they run it and what we hand in
  - The four pass conditions. I gave each a short name so later docs and commits
    can refer to it: "exactly once", "order", "timing" and "intake stays up".
  - What must always hold. Six rules that follow straight from the brief.
  - What's out of scope.
  - Open points. Each one ends with a sentence naming the pass condition it can
    break.

  Open points I added to your nine

  - No time limit on a slow answer.
    - If we stop waiting, we can't know whether port 9000 saved the event.
    - Sending it again may save it twice. Giving up loses it. Port 9000 gives us
      no way to check.
    - I think this is the hardest point in the brief.
  - Two copies of one event arriving at the same moment on separate connections.
    Both need a 2xx, but only one may be stored.
  - Timestamps finer than microseconds. Postgres keeps microseconds only. Finer
    digits in created_at would be rounded, and two events could tie.
  - Bad input at intake, such as broken JSON, missing fields or an unreadable
    created_at.
    - A 2xx for an event we can't send breaks our promise.
    - A 4xx for an event Tequity thinks is valid loses it.
  - Start-up order. Events may arrive before Postgres or the API is ready.
  - Other new details inside your points:
    - The 30,000-event figure for a five-minute outage.
    - The point that we only see recovery by trying to send.

  Things to check

  - CLAUDE.md was missing from disk when I started, though it was still in the
    last commit.
    - The tool's safety check blocked git restore.
    - So I rebuilt the file from the committed text and added the one line. The
      diff shows 2 added lines (the new line and a blank line) and nothing else
      changed.
  - The doc is 968 words, against your target of about 800.
    - The JSON payload and headings take about 60 of those.
    - Most of the rest is the thirteen open points. I'd keep them, but I can cut
      further.
  - One rule may count as a design choice.
    - It's under "what must always hold": a user's event goes out only after all
      their earlier events are saved.
    - I put it there because the order condition seems to force it. Move it to
      the open points if you'd rather decide it later.
  - I didn't add README or decisions.md entries. This commit records the
    problem, not a decision.

> problem.md is good. now lets take the open points one by one, first one is
  how our containers reach 9000 on the host. use superpowers:brainstorming, and
  ecc:docker-patterns for the docker side only. give me the options with trade
  offs and the one you would pick, they run plain docker on linux and we are
  on windows with docker desktop so it has to work on both. dont write a spec
  file and dont commit anything, wait till i agree. once agreed it goes in
  README assumptions and docs/decisions.md in one commit. anti-ai, bullets in
  reply

● Docker Desktop 4.34+ has an opt-in host networking setting, and I'm checking
  the Docker docs to see what it reaches on Windows. That decides whether this
  option will work on your machine. · summary

● web-fetch(Read Docker host network docs)
  ⎿  Received 148 bytes

› Message from web-fetch
  Source: https://docs.docker.com/engine/network/drivers/host/ (read in full,
  HTTP 200). The Docker Desktop section is on this same page (#docker-desktop),
  so no other page was needed. The page is not Windows-specific; it covers
  Docker Desktop generally.

  1. Container to Windows host localhost:9000: Yes, per the docs. "This feature
  works in both directions. This means you can access a server that is running
  in a container from your host and you can access servers running on your host
  from any container that is started with host networking enabled." The docs'
  example runs `nc localhost 80` inside a `--net=host` container to reach a web
  server on the host's port 80. The page does not say anything Windows-specific
  about this.

  2. Host to host-mode container port via localhost: Yes. The docs' example runs
  `nc -lkv 0.0.0.0 8000` in a `--net=host` container, then says "Port 8000 will
  then be available on the host" and connects with `nc localhost 8000`. TCP and
  UDP are both listed as supported.

  3. Requirements:
  - Docker Desktop 4.34 or later. The page says "Host networking is supported on
  Docker Desktop version 4.34 and later."
  - Sign in to your Docker account in Docker Desktop.
  - Settings > Resources > Network, check "Enable host networking", then "Apply
  and restart".
  - Subscription: the page does not mention any subscription or paid-plan
  requirement.

  4. Limitations (from the Docker Desktop "Limitations" list):
  - Processes inside the container cannot bind to the host's IP addresses,
  because the container has no direct access to the host's interfaces.
  - Works at layer 4: "network protocols that operate below TCP or UDP are not
  supported."
  - "This feature doesn't work with Enhanced Container Isolation enabled."
  - "Only Linux containers are supported. Host networking does not work with
  Windows containers."
  Also, the general host-mode note: `-p`/`--publish`/`-P`/`--publish-all` are
  ignored with the warning "Published ports are discarded when using host
  network mode".

  5. Linux Docker Engine caveats:
  - The container shares the host's network namespace, is "not isolated from the
  Docker host", and gets no IP address of its own.
  - Port mappings are ignored (see the warning above).
  - Performance benefit: no NAT and no "userland-proxy" per port.
  - Port 80 example requires that port to be free on the host.
  - The Linux prerequisites section says "The host networking driver only works
  on Linux hosts, and as an opt-in feature in Docker Desktop version 4.34 and
  later." This sits oddly with the Docker Desktop support stated elsewhere on
  the page. Treat the page's Desktop support as stated, but note the
  inconsistency.
  - Swarm services: with `--network host`, a service container binding port 80
  means "only one service container can run on a given swarm node."

● The problem

  - Tequity's brief puts their endpoint at localhost:9000 on the grading machine
    (plain Docker on Linux).
  - Inside a container, localhost means that container. Our workers need some
    other route to the machine's own port 9000.
  - Their load generator also posts to localhost:8080 on the machine, so our
    intake API has to be reachable there.
  - The brief doesn't say which network address their endpoint listens on. That
    turns out to matter most.
    - Some servers accept connections only from the same machine (they bind to
      127.0.0.1).
    - Others accept from anywhere.
    - Python web servers such as Flask and uvicorn default to the first kind.

  Option A: host networking for the API and workers

  - How: set network_mode: host on those services in compose. The containers
    then share the machine's network.
    - localhost:9000 inside the container is the machine's own port 9000.
    - Our API listens straight on the machine's port 8080.
  - On Linux:
    - It reaches their endpoint whatever address it listens on.
    - The machine's firewall has no say, because the traffic never leaves the
      machine's own network.
    - The URL in our code is exactly the brief's: http://localhost:9000/events.
  - On Windows: Docker Desktop has supported this since version 4.34, but only
    once it's switched on.
    - Sign in to Docker Desktop.
    - Go to Settings, then Resources, then Network, and tick "Enable host
      networking".
    - Enhanced Container Isolation (a Docker Desktop security mode) has to stay
      off.
    - Docker's docs say it works both ways: the container reaches the machine's
      localhost, and the machine reaches the container's ports.
  - Costs:
    - Our ports take the machine's ports directly. If anything else on their box
      already uses 8080, we fail to start. The brief needs 8080 from us either
      way, so that risk exists in every option.
    - Containers on host networking can't find other services by name. Postgres
      becomes a localhost address too, which raises a port question I'd settle
      next.
    - On your laptop it fails until the setting is on. You fix that once, by
      hand.

  Option B: the normal compose network plus a name for the host

  - How:
    - Add extra_hosts: ["host.docker.internal:host-gateway"] to the workers.
      That gives the host machine the name host.docker.internal inside the
      container.
    - Post to http://host.docker.internal:9000/events.
    - Publish port 8080 the normal way.
  - On Windows: it works with no setting changes, since Docker Desktop provides
    that name already.
  - On Linux: it needs Docker Engine 20.10 or later, which is from 2020.
  - Costs:
    - It fails if their endpoint accepts connections only from the same machine.
      Traffic from the container comes from the Docker network, so it would be
      refused.
    - It can fail if their machine runs a firewall. Ubuntu's ufw, for example,
      drops traffic from Docker networks to the host by default.
    - We can't see or fix either of these on their box.

  Options I dropped

  - Hard-coding Docker's host address (172.17.0.1).
    - It has Option B's risks.
    - It's also wrong on Docker Desktop.
  - A setting that switches between A and B.
    - The graders edit nothing, so the default must already be right for Linux.
      The switch would only help us.
    - CLAUDE.md's "Small" rule argues against adding config.
  - A forwarding container on host networking.
    - Our workers would still need to reach it across the Docker network.
    - So Option B's problems come back.

  My pick: Option A

  - Where each option breaks decides it.
    - A can only break on your laptop, and turning one setting on fixes it.
    - B breaks on their grading box, through an endpoint address or firewall we
      can't see. If that happens, nothing gets delivered.
  - A also matches the brief exactly: our code uses the same localhost address
    the brief gives.

  What I checked, and what's still unproven

  - Your Docker command-line tool is version 28.4.0, which ships with Docker
    Desktop well past 4.34.
    - Docker Desktop wasn't running, so I couldn't read the engine version.
  - The host networking setting doesn't appear in your Docker Desktop settings
    file. It's probably off.
  - Docker's own docs contradict themselves.
    - One section says host networking works only on Linux hosts.
    - Another section of the same page says Docker Desktop 4.34 and later
      support it.
    - That's one more reason to test it here.
  - Nothing is tested yet. Once you start Docker Desktop and turn the setting
    on, I'd run a throwaway check that touches no repo files:
    - A small web server on your Windows localhost:9000, with a host-networking
      container fetching from it.
    - A host-networking container listening on 8080, with Windows fetching from
      it.
  - I can't test plain Linux from here. Option A on Linux is Docker's standard,
    documented behaviour.

  Waiting on you

  - Which do you want, A or B?
  - Shall I run that throwaway check before the commit?
  - Nothing is written or committed. Once you agree, the README assumption and
    the docs/decisions.md entry go in one commit.

  Sources:
  - Docker docs: Host network driver
    (https://docs.docker.com/engine/network/drivers/host/)
  - Docker docs: Docker Desktop networking
    (https://docs.docker.com/desktop/networking)

  Step 1: a container fetches from a Windows server that only accepts local
  connections

  The test server, started on Windows:
  python -m http.server 9000 --bind 127.0.0.1

  Before involving Docker, I checked the server from Windows. It answered, as
  its own log shows:
  127.0.0.1 - - [18/Nov/2026 21:14:02] "GET / HTTP/1.1" 200 -

  The check itself:
  $ docker run --rm --network host curlimages/curl:8.10.1 -sS -i --max-time 10
  http://localhost:9000/
  curl: (7) Failed to connect to localhost port 9000 after 0 ms: Could not
  connect to server
  exit code: 7

  - Result: fail. The container couldn't connect at all, and the server never
    saw a request from it.
  - "after 0 ms" means the connection was turned away instantly. A firewall
    dropping the traffic would usually make curl wait until it gave up.

  A first attempt that didn't count

  - Before the run above, I made one attempt with a broken script.
  - head -8 cut the command off while Docker was still downloading the curl
    image, so no request was ever sent.
  - I fixed only the script (downloaded the image first, removed head) and ran
    step 1 again. The run above is that second attempt.
  - Nothing about the test or your machine changed between the two.

  What this tells us

  - On your laptop, a host-networking container can't reach a Windows server
    that only accepts connections from Windows itself. That's exactly the case
    we picked option A for.
  - This result is from Docker Desktop on Windows. It says nothing about plain
    Docker on Linux, where host networking really is the machine's own network.
    That part is still untested.
  - I don't know the cause yet. Possible explanations, none checked:
    - Docker Desktop's host networking may only forward to Windows servers that
      accept connections from anywhere (bound to 0.0.0.0), not to ones bound to
      127.0.0.1.
    - The setting may not have fully applied, even though it was ticked.
  - I've cleaned up: the test server is stopped and the container removed
    itself.

  - Find the cause first. Run the same test against a Windows server bound to
    0.0.0.0. If that one works, host networking is on, and the limit is about
    127.0.0.1 servers on Windows.
  - Revisit the choice between A and B with this result in hand.
  - I'll do neither until you say.

● The checks passed for the two cases that mirror the grading machine, and the
  three commits are in. Below that are my picks for the language and every
  remaining open point. Nothing for those is written or committed.

  The checks

  Step 1: one host-network container reaches a server in another, bound to
  127.0.0.1 only. Pass.
  $ docker run -d --name hn-srv9000 --network host python:3.12-alpine python -m
  http.server 9000 --bind 127.0.0.1
  $ docker run --rm --network host curlimages/curl:8.10.1 -sS -i --retry 10
  --retry-connrefused --retry-delay 1 --max-time 30 http://localhost:9000/
  curl: (7) Failed to connect to localhost port 9000 after 0 ms: Could not
  connect to server
  HTTP/1.0 200 OK
  Server: SimpleHTTP/0.6 Python/3.12.16
  exit code: 0
  - The single curl: (7) is the first try, made before the server had started.
    curl retried and got the 200.
  - netstat -tln in the server container showed 127.0.0.1:9000 ... LISTEN, so it
    really was bound to 127.0.0.1 only.

  Step 2: Windows reaches a host-network container on 8080. Fail, which you said
  to note and carry on past.
  $ docker run -d --name hn-srv8080 --network host python:3.12-alpine python -m
  http.server 8080
  $ curl.exe -sS -i --max-time 10 http://localhost:8080/        (on Windows)
  curl: (7) Failed to connect to localhost port 8080 after 2237 ms: Could not
  connect to server
  - My script also printed "exit code: 0" here. That number is wrong: it was
    read after another command had reset it. curl's error line is the result.
  - So the load generator goes in a container, as you said.

  Step 3: Postgres on host networking, port 54329, local connections only. Pass.
  $ docker run -d --name hn-pg --network host -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=check
  postgres:16-alpine -p 54329 -c listen_addresses=127.0.0.1
  $ docker run --rm --network host -e PGPASSWORD=check postgres:16-alpine sh -c
  "until pg_isready ...; psql -h 127.0.0.1 -p 54329 -U postgres -c \"select
  current_setting('port'), current_setting('listen_addresses'),
  inet_server_addr()\""
   port  |  listen   | connected_to
   54329 | 127.0.0.1 | 127.0.0.1
  exit code: 0
  - netstat also showed something else on 127.0.0.1:5432. It's
    library-desk-postgres-1, another project's Postgres on your laptop. That's
    exactly the clash port 54329 avoids.
  - All the test containers are removed.

  The commits

  - 900044d: run the API and worker on host networking.
    - The decisions entry says plainly that the 127.0.0.1 case failed on Docker
      Desktop for Windows.
    - It also says the case still needs proving on Linux, and that we prove it
      when the test rig runs there.
  - cd870ce: run Postgres on host networking at 127.0.0.1:54329.
  - c3f4ee2: run the test rig in host-network containers, because of the Windows
    limit.
  - Each commit adds a README assumption and a docs/decisions.md entry. Both
    files are new; the first commit created them.
  - I rewrote one commit message before moving on: the first version of 900044d
    used a banned word. It hadn't been pushed.

  0. Language and runtime

  - Options:
    - Go: one small program file with no runtime to install, built-in web server
      and client, cheap parallel tasks (goroutines).
    - Python with asyncio: the least code to write, fast enough for 100 a
      second, but more packages to pin.
    - Node: much like Python.
    - Java or Kotlin: bigger images and slower starts for no gain.
  - My pick: Go.
    - A goroutine per user in flight fits the per-user design below.
    - Its HTTP client gives exact control of timeouts, and timeouts decide when
      a send's outcome is unknown.
    - The API, worker and test rig can all be one small binary.
  - If you'd rather read and review Python, it works too. Say so.
  - Postgres 16, with the image version pinned, since that's what step 3 tested.

  How the worker pieces fit together

  - 1. One worker process.
    - Options: one process running many users at once, or several processes that
      split users between them using locks in Postgres.
    - My pick: one process.
      - 100 events a second is light work.
      - With one process, nothing has to coordinate between workers.
      - If it crashes, Docker restarts it.
  - The data: one events table.
    - Each row holds the event_id (unique), user_id, created_at, the body
      exactly as received, an arrival number (for ties) and delivered_at.
    - delivered_at is the only record of progress. Anything without it gets
      sent.
  - The loop:
    - Every 200 ms the worker asks Postgres which users have an unsent event
      that arrived at least 1 second ago (see point 3).
    - It skips users already being sent and users who are paused.
    - For each remaining user, up to 200 at a time, it sends that user's oldest
      unsent event and waits for the answer.
    - On a 200 it sets delivered_at, commits, and moves straight to the user's
      next event.
    - Any other answer pauses that user (points 8 to 11).
  - Kept in memory: which users are in flight or paused. Neither has to survive
    a restart. After a restart, the worst case is one extra 429.

  The per-user maths (open point: one request in flight per user)

  - One event at a time per user is forced.
    - If we sent event 2 before event 1 is saved, and event 1 got a 500, event 2
      would be saved first and the order would break.
    - So one user's speed is at most 1 ÷ (the endpoint's answer time + our time
      per event).
  - Our time per event: one database commit on localhost, about 2 ms. That's my
    estimate; the test rig will measure it.

  ┌──────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Endpoint answer time │ Most events per second for one user │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2 ms                 │ 250                                 │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 10 ms                │ about 83                            │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 50 ms                │ about 19                            │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 200 ms               │ about 5                             │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1 s                  │ about 1                             │
  └──────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘

  - Normal running:
    - Say the heaviest user sends 30 of the 100 events a second.
    - The endpoint then has to answer in about 31 ms or less, or that user falls
      further behind every second.
  - After an outage (open point: backlog):
    - A user sending r events a second, after an outage of D seconds, needs r ×
      (D + 300) ÷ 300 events a second to clear everything within 5 minutes.
    - After a 5-minute outage that's 2r. The heaviest user, at 30, needs 60 a
      second, so the endpoint must answer in about 14 ms.
    - Across all users that's 60,000 events in 300 seconds, 200 a second. With
      200 users in flight, even 1-second answers give 200 a second in total.
    - So the total isn't the limit. The heaviest user is.
  - What I'd write in the README:
    - We assume the endpoint answers in about 14 ms or less when healthy.
      Requests over localhost usually take a few ms.
    - If the endpoint rate limits us below 200 a second during recovery, no
      design meets 5 minutes after a 5-minute outage.

  Intake points

  - 2. Events arriving out of created_at order.
    - Options:
      - Send at once, and accept that a late earlier event comes out of order.
      - Hold each event 1 second after it arrives, so a slightly late one slots
        in ahead.
      - Drop late events, which breaks exactly once.
    - My pick: hold for 1 second.
      - That catches reordering between parallel connections, and costs 1 of the
        30 seconds.
      - An earlier event that arrives after a later one has already gone out
        still gets sent, late, and logged. One order miss beats a lost event.
  - 3. Timestamps finer than microseconds.
    - Options:
      - Store created_at as a Postgres timestamp, which keeps microseconds.
      - Store it as a nanosecond number, which takes more parsing code.
    - My pick: the Postgres timestamp.
      - Ties are broken by arrival order.
      - README assumption: created_at has at most 6 decimal places. The brief's
        example has 3.
  - 4. jsonb changes the body.
    - My pick: store the body as raw bytes (bytea) and send those exact bytes,
      with Content-Type: application/json.
    - We still parse the JSON once, to check it and pull out the user_id and
      created_at columns.
    - The alternative, rebuilding the body from jsonb, risks a different body.
  - 5. Two copies arriving at the same moment.
    - My pick: event_id is the primary key, and the insert uses ON CONFLICT DO
      NOTHING.
    - Both copies get a 200. Postgres makes sure only one row exists.
  - 6. Same event_id, different body.
    - Options:
      - Keep the first and answer 200.
      - Answer 409 (a "conflict" error).
      - Overwrite with the new body if the old one isn't sent yet.
    - My pick: keep the first, answer 200, log it.
      - The brief says resends keep the same event.
      - A 409 might make their load generator retry forever, or count the event
        as refused.
  - 7. Bad input.
    - My pick: answer 400 for anything we can't send:
      - a body that isn't a JSON object
      - a missing or empty event_id
      - a missing or empty user_id
      - a missing or unreadable created_at
    - Bodies over 1 MB get a 413 ("too large").
    - We don't check type or data. We just pass them on.
    - A 400 makes no promise, so it can't break exactly once.
  - Intake answer: 200, only after Postgres has committed the row.

  Delivery points

  - 8. Answers outside the table.
    - My pick:
      - Any 2xx means saved.
      - Any 5xx, 3xx or other 4xx means not saved: retry. We don't follow
        redirects.
      - Connection refused means the endpoint is down and nothing was sent:
        retry.
      - A dropped connection, or no answer after we sent the request, means
        unknown (point 9).
    - On other 4xx answers:
      - Retrying could block that user forever.
      - Skipping the event breaks exactly once.
      - I'd retry and log loudly, since a 4xx most likely means a bug of ours
        that needs fixing.
  - Retry timing: wait 0.5 seconds, then double each time, up to 5 seconds.
    - With that cap we notice recovery within 5 seconds, well inside the 5
      minutes.
  - 9. Slow answer and unknown outcome.
    - Options:
      - Send again: wrong only if the lost try was in fact saved.
      - Give up: wrong only if it wasn't.
      - Either way, a wrong guess breaks exactly once.
    - My pick: wait up to 60 seconds for an answer, then send again if the
      outcome is unknown.
      - The brief says slow answers are still in progress, so a long wait turns
        most of them into a clear 200.
      - The most common remaining unknown is the endpoint dying mid-request, and
        that most likely saved nothing.
  - 10. We crash mid-send.
    - My pick: the same rule. The event has no delivered_at, so it gets sent
      again.
    - On a normal stop, the worker stops starting new sends and waits for the
      ones in progress. That takes a compose grace period of 70 seconds.
    - A hard kill can still cause a duplicate. README assumption.
  - 11. Retry-After scope.
    - Options:
      - Pause every user on any 429.
      - Pause only the user who got it.
    - My pick: pause only that user.
      - If the limit covers the whole endpoint, each user that hits it pauses
        itself, and everything slows down on its own.
      - If the limit is per user, nobody else waits for nothing.
    - A missing or broken Retry-After counts as a 1-second wait.

  Start-up

  - 12. Start-up order.
    - My pick:
      - Postgres gets a health check (pg_isready), and the API and worker wait
        for it (depends_on: service_healthy).
      - The tables come from one SQL file that Postgres runs on first start.
      - All services use restart: unless-stopped.
    - README assumption: Tequity starts sending once docker compose up shows us
      running. Posts in the few seconds before that get refused, which carries
      no 2xx promise.

  Other things

  - An untracked file appeared in the repo root:
    2026-11-18-211427-command-messagesuperpowersusing-superpowersc.txt. It looks
    like a chat export. I didn't create it and left it alone.
  - Reply OK or change it, point by point, using the numbers.

> ok on 0, 1, the per user maths and the 14ms assumption, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 10,
  12.

  changes:
  2, the hold has a order bug as written. it must look at the users oldest
  unsent event by created_at, if that one arrived under 1s ago the whole user
  waits. never send a later event because the earlier one is still inside its
  hold.
  9, no overall timeout on the request. brief says slow means still coming and
  a 200 means saved, so timeout plus resend is the one way we double save on a
  slow but alive endpoint. short connect timeout is fine, refused means not
  sent. turn on tcp keepalive so a dead connection still errors, that error is
  the unknown case and we resend. adjust 10s grace period to match.
  11, a 429 pauses all sending for Retry-After, whole endpoint. brief puts rate
  limiting next to down and slow as an endpoint state, and per user pausing

● I've made the 14 commits, one per decision: points 0 to 12, plus the per-user
  maths as its own commit. Only README.md and docs/decisions.md were staged, so
  the chat export is still untracked. Nothing was tested; these are design

  The commits, oldest first

    progress.

  - 33c08a9: 2. Hold a user while its oldest unsent event is under 1 second old.
  - e3b5b6b: 3. Store created_at as a Postgres timestamp and break ties by
    arrival order.

  - 2c176c2: 5. Make event_id the primary key, so a repeated post is stored once
    and still gets a 200.

    one.
  - 192a4b6: 7. Answer 400 to events we can't send, and 200 only after the
    commit.
  - 4500269: 8. Treat any 2xx as saved and anything else as not saved, retrying
    from 0.5 up to 5 seconds.
  - 7d0fa0d: 9. Wait for slow answers with no overall timeout, and resend only
    when the connection is lost after sending.
  - df32080: 10. Give in-progress sends 20 seconds to finish on a normal stop,
    and resend whatever was cut off.
  - 1663034: 11. Pause all sending for Retry-After on any 429.
  - 9aa28d6: 12. Start the API and worker only once Postgres is healthy, and
    restart every service unless stopped.

  Your three changes, as written

  - 2. The hold
    - The whole user waits while their oldest unsent event, by created_at,
      arrived under 1 second ago.
    - The worker checks again before every send. So an earlier event that turns
      up partway through stops the loop until its second is up, then goes out
      first.
  - 9. No overall timeout
    - There's a 2-second connect timeout. A connect that fails sent nothing, so
      it counts as not saved.
    - The entry also says Go's HTTP client must never resend a POST by itself.
      Go only retries a POST when nothing at all was written, so that's safe.
  - 11. A 429 pauses the whole endpoint
    - Sends already in progress carry on.
    - A second 429 during a pause pushes the end out to whichever end is later.

  Calls I made that you didn't specify

  - Grace period: 20 seconds, down from my earlier 70.
    - With no request timeout, nothing sets a natural length for it.
    - While the worker stops, no user moves forward, and the brief allows 30
      seconds from our 2xx.
    - 20 seconds lets most slow answers finish and leaves time to restart and
      catch up.
  - Keepalive settings
    - Probes start after 10 seconds of silence and repeat every 5 seconds.
    - The connection fails after 3 missed probes, so a vanished connection
      errors after about 25 seconds.
  - Keepalive's limit, written into the entry
    - It only catches a connection whose other end has vanished.
    - An endpoint that's slow but alive still answers the probes, so we keep
      waiting, which is what you asked for.
  - Your corrections are on record. The entries for points 2, 9 and 10 each name
    the earlier version and say why it changed, so the history shows how we got
    there.
  - The same banned-word slip didn't happen again. The script checked every
    message, README line and entry before committing anything.

  Next

  - Every open point in docs/problem.md now has a decision.
  - What's left before product code: agree how the test rig behaves (the fake
    endpoint, the load generator and the checker), since CLAUDE.md requires that
    before the rig is built.

      151  The connect step has a 2-second timeout. A refused or failed c
           onnect means the request was never sent, so the event is not s
           aved and is retried. TCP keepalive (the system sending small p
           robes on an idle connection to check its other end is still th
           ere) is on: probes start after 10 seconds of silence, repeat e
           very 5 seconds, and the connection fails after 3 missed probes
           . Keepalive catches a connection whose other end has vanished
           without closing it. An endpoint that is alive but slow still a
           nswers the probes, so its request keeps waiting.

          -id-request, and that most likely saved nothing. Giving up was

      121  ## 2026-11-18: A user waits while its oldest unsent event is u
           nder 1 second old
      122 
      123  The worker looks at each user's oldest unsent event by created
           _at. If that event arrived less than 1 second ago, the whole u
           ser waits. No later event of that user goes out while an earli
           er one is still inside its hold. The same check runs before ev
           ery send in a user's loop, so an earlier event that arrives mi
           d-loop stops the loop until its second has passed, and then go
           es out first.

● Update(docs\decisions.md)
  ⎿  Added 3 lines, removed 1 line
      160 
      161  The brief's table covers 200, 500, 429, no connection and slow
           . For everything else: any 2xx means saved. Any 5xx, any 3xx a
           nd any 4xx other than 429 mean not saved, and redirects are no
           t followed. A refused connection or a failed connect means the
            request was never sent, so not saved. A connection lost after
            the request went out is the unknown case, covered in its own
           entry, and 429 has its own entry too.
      162 
      163 -A not-saved event is retried after 0.5 seconds, then 1, 2 and 
          -4, then every 5 seconds. With that cap the worker notices reco
          -very within about 5 seconds, well inside the 5 minutes the bri
          -ef allows.                                                    
      163 +A not-saved event is retried after 20 ms, and the wait doubles
          + on each failure in a row (40, 80, 160 ms and so on) up to 5 s
          +econds. It goes back to 20 ms after a success. A single 500 th
          +en costs about 20 ms, and a long failure backs off to one try 
          +every 5 seconds per user within about 10 seconds. With that ca
          +p the worker notices recovery within about 5 seconds, well ins
          +ide the 5 minutes the brief allows. A refused connection follo
          +ws the same schedule: on localhost it costs well under 1 ms, s
          +o a 20 ms start is as cheap there as for a 500.               
      164 
      165 +Changed 2026-11-19 after review. The first version started at 
          +0.5 seconds. With 2% of attempts answered 500, those waits alo
          +ne made the heaviest user too slow to clear a 5-minute outage;
          + the numbers are in the 10 ms entry.                          
      166 +                                                              
      167  A 4xx other than 429 most likely means a bug of ours. Retrying

      122 
      123  The worker looks at each user's oldest unsent event by created
           _at. If that event arrived less than 1 second ago, the whole u
           ser waits. No later event of that user goes out while an earli
           er one is still inside its hold. The same check runs before ev
           ery send in a user's loop, so an earlier event that arrives mi
           d-loop stops the loop until its second has passed, and then go
           es out first.
      124 
      125 +"Arrived" means `received_at`, set by Postgres's clock when in
          +take inserts the row. created_at is the load generator's clock
          + and only decides order. The hold is applied after picking eac
          +h user's oldest unsent event, never in a WHERE before the pick
          +. Filtering first would skip a user's held oldest event and pi
          +ck their second one, which is the order bug described below. T
          +he poll query:                                                
      126 +                                                              
      127 +```sql                                                        
      128 +SELECT user_id FROM (                                         
      129 +  SELECT DISTINCT ON (user_id) user_id, received_at           
      130 +  FROM events WHERE delivered_at IS NULL                      
      131 +  ORDER BY user_id, created_at, seq) o                        
      132 +WHERE received_at < now() - interval '1 second' AND user_id <>
          + ALL($1);                                                     
      133 +```                                                           
      134 +                                                              
      135 +`$1` is the list of users the worker is already sending for. (
          +Added 2026-11-19 after review: the first version named no arri
          +val time.)                                                    
      136 +                                                              
      137  Events can reach us out of created_at order, for example over
           parallel connections. The hold lets a slightly late earlier ev
           ent take its place, and costs 1 of the 30 seconds the brief al
           lows.
      138 
      139  The first version of this choice held each event on its own ar
           rival time. That had an order bug: a later event could go out
           while an earlier one was still held. The hold now applies to t
           he user, keyed on its oldest unsent event.

● Item 5: a database error after a 200. The finding is correct. Once the
  endpoint has said 200, the event is saved, and a failed delivered_at update
  must never turn into a resend.

  ⎿  Added 2 lines
      76 
      77  Events go in one `events` table: event_id as the primary key, u
          ser_id, created_at, the body as received, `received_at timestam
          ptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()` (when the event arrived, by Postgre
          s's clock), `seq bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY` (an arriv
          al number used to break ties), and `delivered_at`. A single wor
          ker process sends for all users. Every 200 ms it asks Postgres
          which users have an event ready to go (the 1-second hold decide
          s "ready"). It skips users it is already sending for, and start
          s a loop for each of the others, up to 200 users at a time. A l
          oop sends the user's oldest unsent event, waits for the answer,
           and on a 2xx sets `delivered_at`, commits and moves on to the
          next one.
      78 
      79 +After a 2xx the event is saved, whatever happens next on our si
         +de. If the `UPDATE ... SET delivered_at` fails, the loop retrie
         +s only that UPDATE until it commits, and never sends the event 
         +again because of a database error. The user's loop waits until 
         +the commit goes through. Only a crash before that commit leads 
         +to a resend. (Added 2026-11-19 after review.)                  
      80 +                                                               
      81  The worker keeps in memory which users are in flight, plus any
          pause or retry wait. None of that has to survive a restart. Aft
          er one, anything without `delivered_at` is sent again, and the
          worst a forgotten wait costs is one extra 500 or 429.
      82 
      83  The other option was several worker processes splitting users b
          etween them through row locks or leases in Postgres. At 100 eve
          nts a second one process has plenty of room, and with one there
           is nothing to coordinate. If it dies, Docker restarts it. The
          limit of 200 users in flight comes from the per-user maths: eve
          n at 1-second answers it gives 200 events a second in total, th
          e rate needed to clear a 5-minute outage.

● I'm leaving the endpoint-never-answers fix out and adding only a warning for

● Update(docs\decisions.md)  ⎿  Added 2 lines
      186 
      187  The connect step has a 2-second timeout. A refused or failed c
           onnect means the request was never sent, so the event is not s
           aved and is retried. TCP keepalive (the system sending small p
           robes on an idle connection to check its other end is still th
           ere) is on: probes start after 10 seconds of silence, repeat e
           very 5 seconds, and the connection fails after 3 missed probes
           . Keepalive catches a connection whose other end has vanished
           without closing it. An endpoint that is alive but slow still a
           nswers the probes, so its request keeps waiting.
      188 
      189 +On localhost the endpoint's own kernel answers keepalive probe
          +s, so an endpoint that holds a connection open and never answe
          +rs keeps that send, and its user, waiting for good. We accept 
          +that. A ceiling followed by a resend is the timeout-then-resen
          +d this entry rules out, and the brief's table has no endpoint 
          +that never answers: a slow answer "is still coming". Only that
          + one user waits; the other send slots carry on. To make such a
          + send visible, the worker logs a warning with the event_id and
          + user_id for any send in flight over 60 seconds, and again eac
          +h further minute. (Added 2026-11-19 after review, which propos
          +ed a 120-second ceiling.)                                     
      190 +                                                              
      191  The outcome is decided by the status line alone. Once a status
            line has come back, a 200 means saved, and any error in the r
           est of the answer, while it comes in or is drained, is ignored
           ; the body is drained only so the connection can be used again
           . A 200 followed by a cut body must never count as unknown, or
            the event would be resent and saved twice. (Added 2026-11-19
           after review.)
      192 
      193  A connection that fails or closes after the request went out,
           before a status line comes back, is the unknown case. We send
           the event again. The most likely cause is the endpoint dying m
           id-request, and that most likely saved nothing. Giving up was
           the other option, and it is wrong whenever nothing was saved.
           The HTTP client must never resend a POST on its own: Go's clie
           nt only retries a POST when nothing at all was written, which
           is safe.

● I've applied three of the fixes (the 120-second grace period, the
  uncancellable send context, and exec-form ENTRYPOINT) but pushed back on
  stamping attempt_started_at before every send, since my pgbench run showed it
  adds about 1 ms per event and only buys a crash log line. I'm keeping a

      199 -When the worker is told to stop, it starts no new sends and wa
          -its for the ones in progress. Compose's `stop_grace_period` fo
          -r the worker is 20 seconds; after that the worker is killed. A
          -ny send cut off, by that kill or by a crash, has no `delivered
          -_at`, so it is sent again on restart and may be saved twice. A
          - 200 that arrives just before a crash, ahead of the commit of 
          -`delivered_at`, has the same result.                          
      199 +Changed 2026-11-19 after review. The first version gave 20 sec
          +onds.                                                         
      200 
      201 -Sends have no overall timeout, so the grace period cannot wait
          - for every send to end on its own. While the worker is stoppin
          -g, no user moves forward, and the brief allows 30 seconds from
          - our 2xx. 20 seconds lets most slow answers finish and leaves 
          -time to restart and catch up. The earlier plan was 70 seconds,
          - sized to a 60-second request timeout that no longer exists.  
      201 +When the worker gets SIGTERM, it starts no new sends and waits
          + for the ones in progress, then exits as soon as the last one 
          +has its answer and its `delivered_at` write. Compose's `stop_g
          +race_period` for the worker is 120 seconds; after that the wor
          +ker is killed. Any send cut off, by that kill or by a crash, h
          +as no `delivered_at`, so it is sent again on restart and may b
          +e saved twice. A 200 that arrives just before a crash, ahead o
          +f the commit of `delivered_at`, has the same result.          
      202 
      203 +A short grace period is a timeout-then-resend in another form:
          + a slow send killed at the deadline is resent on restart, whic
          +h is the double save the no-timeout entry rules out. With 120 
          +seconds, only a send slower than two minutes is cut off. The w
          +orker exits as soon as its sends finish, so the full 120 secon
          +ds is spent only when a send is that slow, and then the stop i
          +s slow instead of risking a double save.                      
      204 +                                                              
      205 +Two things in the Go code and image make this work. The HTTP c
          +all and the `delivered_at` write run on a context that SIGTERM
          + does not cancel (`context.WithoutCancel`), so the stop signal
          + cannot abort the send we are waiting for or the write after i
          +t. And the image starts the binary with an exec-form `ENTRYPOI
          +NT ["/relay", ...]`, so SIGTERM reaches Go at all; with the sh
          +ell form, `/bin/sh` is process 1 and does not pass the signal 
          +on.                                                           
      206 +                                                              
      207 +The review also proposed stamping `attempt_started_at` on each
          + event before sending it, so that a restart could log every ev
          +ent whose outcome is unknown. We left that out. pgbench measur
          +ed the stamp, even with a non-durable commit, at about 1.0 to 
          +1.2 ms per event: BEGIN, SET LOCAL synchronous_commit = off, U
          +PDATE and COMMIT. That would raise our time per event from abo
          +ut 5 ms to 6 ms and lower the endpoint speed the heaviest user
          + needs from about 10.7 ms to about 9.7 ms (see the 10 ms entry
          +), all for a log line after a crash. At startup the worker log
          +s how many events are unsent instead.                         
      208 +                                                              
      209 +The earlier plans were 70 seconds, sized to a 60-second reques
          +t timeout that no longer exists, and then 20 seconds, sized to
          + the brief's 30-second window. The 20 seconds was dropped for 
          +the reason above.                                             
      210 +                                                              
      211  ## 2026-11-18: A 429 pauses all sending for Retry-After
      212 
      213  On any 429 the worker starts no new sends to the endpoint, for
            any user, until Retry-After seconds have passed. If the heade
           r is missing or is not a whole number, the pause is 1 second.
           Sends already in progress carry on. A later 429 during a pause
            moves its end to whichever end is later. The event that got t
           he 429 was not saved and goes out again after the pause.

● Update(docs\decisions.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
      200 
      201  When the worker gets SIGTERM, it starts no new sends and waits
            for the ones in progress, then exits as soon as the last one
           has its answer and its `delivered_at` write. Compose's `stop_g
           race_period` for the worker is 120 seconds; after that the wor
           ker is killed. Any send cut off, by that kill or by a crash, h
           as no `delivered_at`, so it is sent again on restart and may b
           e saved twice. A 200 that arrives just before a crash, ahead o
           f the commit of `delivered_at`, has the same result.
      202 
      203 -A short grace period is a timeout-then-resend in another form:
          - a slow send killed at the deadline is resent on restart, whic
          -h is the double save the no-timeout entry rules out. With 120 
          -seconds, only a send slower than two minutes is cut off. The w
          -orker exits as soon as its sends finish, so the full 120 secon
          -ds is spent only when a send is that slow, and then the stop i
          -s slow instead of risking a double save.                      
      203 +A short grace period is a timeout-then-resend in another form:
          + a slow send killed at the deadline is resent on restart, whic
          +h is the double save the no-timeout entry rules out. With 120 
          +seconds, only a send slower than two minutes is cut off. The w
          +orker exits as soon as its sends finish, so the full 120 secon
          +ds is spent only when a send is that slow, and then a slow sto
          +p is the price of avoiding a double save.                     
      204 
      205  Two things in the Go code and image make this work. The HTTP c
           all and the `delivered_at` write run on a context that SIGTERM
            does not cancel (`context.WithoutCancel`), so the stop signal
            cannot abort the send we are waiting for or the write after i
           t. And the image starts the binary with an exec-form `ENTRYPOI
           NT ["/relay", ...]`, so SIGTERM reaches Go at all; with the sh
           ell form, `/bin/sh` is process 1 and does not pass the signal
           on.

● Item 8's finding holds: Go's default pool has no connection cap, and the HTTP
  client keeps only 2 idle connections per host, so the 200 sends would mostly
  open new ones. The 20-connection pgbench run puts the worker's throughput at
  about 3,460 steps a second, well above the 200 the outage maths needs.
  · summary

● Update(docs\decisions.md)
  ⎿  Added 2 lines
      78 
      79  After a 2xx the event is saved, whatever happens next on our si
          de. If the `UPDATE ... SET delivered_at` fails, the loop retrie
          s only that UPDATE until it commits, and never sends the event
          again because of a database error. The user's loop waits until
          the commit goes through. Only a crash before that commit leads
          to a resend. (Added 2026-11-19 after review.)
      80 
      81 +Connection pools are set by hand, since Go's defaults do not fi
         +t. Go's database pool has no limit by default, and Postgres all
         +ows 100 connections, so 200 user loops could take them all and 
         +leave intake unable to commit. The worker's pool is capped at 2
         +0 connections and the API's at 30, 50 in all. Each user loop ho
         +lds a connection only for one statement at a time. pgbench with
         + 20 connections ran about 3,460 worker steps a second (mark del
         +ivered, then look up the next event), 17 times the 200 a second
         + the outage maths needs. Go's HTTP client keeps only 2 idle con
         +nections per host by default, so most of 200 sends at once woul
         +d open a new TCP connection each time; the worker sets `MaxIdle
         +ConnsPerHost` to 200. (Added 2026-11-19 after review.)         
      82 +                                                               
      83  The worker keeps in memory which users are in flight, plus any
          pause or retry wait. None of that has to survive a restart. Aft
          er one, anything without `delivered_at` is sent again, and the
          worst a forgotten wait costs is one extra 500 or 429.
      84 
      85  The other option was several worker processes splitting users b
          etween them through row locks or leases in Postgres. At 100 eve
          nts a second one process has plenty of room, and with one there
           is nothing to coordinate. If it dies, Docker restarts it. The
          limit of 200 users in flight comes from the per-user maths: eve
          n at 1-second answers it gives 200 events a second in total, th
          e rate needed to clear a 5-minute outage.

      140 
      141 +The query runs every 200 ms, and the table grows with every de
          +livered row, so it has a partial index that holds unsent rows 
          +only:                                                         
      142 +                                                              
      143 +```sql                                                        
      144 +CREATE INDEX events_pending ON events (user_id, created_at, se
          +q) INCLUDE (received_at) WHERE delivered_at IS NULL;          
      145 +```                                                           
      146 +                                                              
      147 +The index order matches the DISTINCT ON order, and received_at
          + is included, so Postgres answers from the index alone. On a h
          +ost-network Postgres 16 with 1,000 unsent events, `EXPLAIN (AN
          +ALYZE, BUFFERS)` showed:                                      
      148 +

      150 +->  Index Only Scan using events_pending on events (actual tim
          +e=0.022..0.860 rows=999 loops=1)                              
      151 +      Heap Fetches: 0                                         
      152 +Execution Time: 1.758 ms                                      
      153 +```                                                           
      154 +                                                              
      155 +The scan grows with the number of unsent events, about 0.9 ms 
          +per 1,000 here, so a 30,000-event backlog should take around 2
          +5 to 30 ms per poll, well under the 200 ms between polls. That
          + figure is an estimate and the test rig will measure it. (Adde

● I'm applying the parsing fix with one change: a created_at without a time zone
  will be taken as UTC instead of rejected, since rejecting it would drop
  events for no gain. · summary

● Update(docs\decisions.md)
  ⎿  Added 4 lines
      164 
      165  created_at is parsed and stored as a Postgres `timestamptz`, w
           hich keeps microseconds. The brief's example has 3 decimal pla
           ces, and the README assumes at most 6. If finer digits ever ro
           und two events of one user to the same value, they go out in t
           he order they reached us. There is no unique rule on (user_id,
            created_at), so a tie can never cause an event to be refused.
      166 
      167 +Intake parses created_at in Go, never in Postgres. It uses Go'
          +s RFC 3339 layout with fractional seconds (`time.RFC3339Nano`)
          +, which accepts `Z` and offsets such as `+05:30`. If the strin
          +g has no time zone, it is taken as UTC. Postgres would take it
          + in the session's zone, which depends on settings. The parsed 
          +time is cut to microseconds by our code, with a log line whene
          +ver digits are dropped, and passed to Postgres as a `time.Time
          +`. Without the cut, Postgres would round extra digits while Go
          +'s driver cuts them, and two code paths could disagree on orde
          +r. A string that matches neither layout gets a 400.           
      168 +                                                              
      169 +The review proposed refusing a created_at with no time zone. W
          +e take it as UTC. Order only matters within one user, and any 
          +fixed rule for zoneless times keeps that order, while a 400 dr
          +ops the event. (Added 2026-11-19 after review.)               
      170 +                                                              
      171  The other option was storing created_at as a number of nanosec
           onds, which needs our own parsing code for a case the brief's
           example does not suggest.

● Item 12 covers intake errors: the finding is right, since our entry never said
  what a database error should return (a 400 would blame the event, a 200 would

● Update(docs\decisions.md)
  ⎿  Added 2 lines
      192

           tore each event once, user_id and created_at to send in order.
            A 2xx for an event we cannot send would break the brief's pro

          +ed. A 503 makes no promise and asks the load generator to try

          +efused by mistake shows up in the logs. (Added 2026-11-19 afte

      197  ## 2026-11-18: Any 2xx means saved, and anything else means no
           t saved and retried
      198 
      199  The brief's table covers 200, 500, 429, no connection and slow
           . For everything else: any 2xx means saved. Any 5xx, any 3xx a
           nd any 4xx other than 429 mean not saved, and redirects are no
           t followed. A refused connection or a failed connect means the
            request was never sent, so not saved. A connection lost after
            the request went out is the unknown case, covered in its own
           entry, and 429 has its own entry too.

          60 seconds is logged as a warning with its event_id. TCP keepal
          ive (the system checking an idle connection's other end is stil
          l there) makes a connection whose other end has vanished fail.
          That failure, like any connection lost after the request went o
          ut and before a status line came back, means the outcome is unk
          nown, and the event is sent again. The status line alone decide
          s: a 200 means saved even if the rest of the answer is cut off.
      19  - On a normal stop the worker starts no new sends and waits up
          to 120 seconds for the ones in progress, exiting as soon as the
          y finish. The stop signal never cancels a send in progress or i
          ts `delivered_at` write. A send still going after that, or one
          cut short by a hard kill, is sent again on restart and may be s
          aved twice.
      20  - A 429 means the whole endpoint is rate limiting. All new send
          s pause for its Retry-After seconds, and for at least 1 second,
           which also covers a missing header, a value that is not a whol
          e number and `Retry-After: 0`. A 429 does not raise the retry w
          ait.

● The finding on item 14 is confirmed on both counts: a panic in any goroutine
  would take down the whole Go process for every user, and depends_on with
  service_healthy only orders startup, so a crash loop on a database error
  persists after Postgres restarts. I'm moving on to the next item. · summary

● Update(docs\decisions.md)
  ⎿  Added 2 lines
      246 
      247  Postgres keeps its data in a named volume, `pgdata:/var/lib/po
           stgresql/data`, and fsync and synchronous_commit stay at their
            defaults (on). Without a named volume, the postgres image's o
           wn volume is anonymous: after `docker compose down` and `up` t
           he new container gets a new, empty one, and every unsent event
            we answered 2xx for is gone. Only `docker compose down -v` de
           letes the named volume. The init SQL runs only on an empty dat
           a folder, so a change to the schema needs `down -v` to take ef
           fect. (Added 2026-11-19 after review.)
      248 
      249 +`depends_on` only orders `docker compose up`. If Postgres rest
          +arts later on its own, the API and worker get no such wait. So
          + neither process ever exits on a database error. At startup th
          +ey retry the connection until Postgres answers, and while runn
          +ing they log each database error and retry inside the process:
          + intake answers 503 meanwhile, and the worker holds its loops.
          + Each user loop in the worker recovers from a panic, logs it w
          +ith the user_id and event_id, and counts it as a failed attemp
          +t, so the retry wait applies. A panic in one user's loop would
          + otherwise kill the whole process, and the restart would hit t
          +he same row and crash again, stopping every user. `restart: un
          +less-stopped` stays as the last line of defence. (Added 2026-1
          +1-19 after review.)                                           
      250 +                                                              
      251  `docker compose up` starts containers in parallel, and Tequity
           's load generator could post before Postgres or the API is rea
           dy. The README assumes Tequity starts sending once our service
           s show as running. A post refused in the first few seconds got
            no 2xx, so it carries no promise.

           he same row and crash again, stopping every user. `restart: un
           less-stopped` stays as the last line of defence. (Added 2026-1

      256

      258 +                                                              
      259 +## 2026-11-19: Every text file is checked out with LF line end
          +ings                                                          
      260 +                                                              
      261 +The repo has a `.gitattributes` with `* text=auto eol=lf`, so 
          +every text file has LF line endings in every checkout, Windows
          + included; the PDF is detected as binary and left alone. Git o
          +n the Windows laptop this is built on has `core.autocrlf=true`
          +. Without the file, a checkout there turns line endings into C
          +RLF, so a Docker build from that laptop could copy files into 
          +the image that differ from what the graders build on Linux, an
          +d a shell script with CRLF fails outright on Linux. What is co
          +mmitted was already LF (`git ls-files --eol` showed `i/lf` for
          + each text file), so nothing in the history changes. The repo 
          +keeps no shell scripts beyond what compose and the Dockerfile 
          +need inline; the init SQL is the only file Postgres runs. (Add
          +ed after review.)

● The laptop's postgres:16-alpine image is 16.16, so I'm pinning the image to
  postgres:16.16-alpine. Go and runtime versions will be pinned once the
  Dockerfile exists. · summary

● Update(docs\decisions.md)
  ⎿  Added 2 lines
      78 
      79  Python with asyncio was the close second: less code, fast enoug
          h for 100 events a second, but more packages to pin. Node is mu
          ch like Python. Java and Kotlin bring bigger images and slower
          starts for no gain here. Postgres 16 is the version the host ne
          tworking checks ran on.
      80 
      81 +Every image is pinned to an exact patch tag, so the graders bui
         +ld what we tested. Postgres is `postgres:16.16-alpine`: the `16
         +-alpine` tag on the build laptop printed `postgres (PostgreSQL)
         + 16.16` for `postgres --version`. The Go build image and the ru
         +ntime image get exact patch tags when the Dockerfile is written
         +. The Dockerfile is a multi-stage build: a Go stage compiles on
         +e static binary with `CGO_ENABLED=0`, and a small runtime stage
         + holds only that binary. `go.sum` is committed, so the graders'
         + build downloads the same module versions. (Added 2026-11-19 af
         +ter review.)                                                   
      82 +                                                               
      83  ## 2026-11-18: One worker process, with delivered_at as the onl
          y record of progress
      84 
      85  Events go in one `events` table: event_id as the primary key, u
          ser_id, created_at, the body as received, `received_at timestam
          ptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()` (when the event arrived, by Postgre
          s's clock), `seq bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY` (an arriv
          al number used to break ties), and `delivered_at`. A single wor
          ker process sends for all users. Every 200 ms it asks Postgres
          which users have an event ready to go (the 1-second hold decide
          s "ready"). It skips users it is already sending for, and start
          s a loop for each of the others, up to 200 users at a time. A l
          oop sends the user's oldest unsent event, waits for the answer,
           and on a 2xx sets `delivered_at`, commits and moves on to the
          next one.

           time. An event's `delivered_at` is the only record of progress
          : anything without it gets sent. After a 2xx, a failed database
           write of `delivered_at` is retried until it commits, and never
           causes a resend. The worker uses at most 20 database connectio
          ns and the API at most 30, well under Postgres's 100.
      10  - When healthy, Tequity's endpoint answers in about 10 ms or le
          ss, with 500s on at most a few percent of requests. Taking the
          heaviest user as 30 of the 100 events a second, that is the spe
          ed it needs to clear the backlog of a 5-minute outage within 5
          minutes of recovery, counting our own 5 ms per event and the wa
          its before retries. If the endpoint rate limits us below 200 ev
          ents a second during recovery, no design meets that.
      11  - A user's events wait while that user's oldest unsent event, b
          y created_at, arrived less than 1 second ago. Arrival time is `
          received_at`, set by Postgres's clock on insert; created_at is
          the load generator's clock and only decides order. A later even
          t never goes out while an earlier one is still inside its hold.
           An earlier event that arrives after a later one was already se
          nt still goes out, late, and is logged.

           s us below about 200 a second during recovery, nothing meets t
           he 5 minutes after a 5-minute outage.

● Item 20's Retry-After scope is reversed to per-user, since per-user pausing
  holds whether the limit is per user or endpoint-wide, while a global pause
  would freeze everyone whenever one heavy user keeps getting 429s. · summary

      18  - Any 2xx from the endpoint means saved. Any other status, a re
          fused connection or a failed connect means not saved, and the e
          vent is retried after 20 ms, doubling on each failure in a row
          up to 5 seconds. Redirects are not followed. An event the endpo
          int keeps refusing blocks its user; each failure is logged at e
          rror level with a count, and a status line every minute shows u
          nsent events, the oldest one's age, the longest send in flight
          and how many users are blocked.
      19  - A slow answer is always waited for, and an endpoint that neve
          r answers is outside the brief's table. Sends have no overall t
          imeout, only a 2-second connect timeout; a send in flight over
          60 seconds is logged as a warning with its event_id. TCP keepal
          ive (the system checking an idle connection's other end is stil
          l there) makes a connection whose other end has vanished fail.
          That failure, like any connection lost after the request went o
          ut and before a status line came back, means the outcome is unk
          nown, and the event is sent again. The status line alone decide
          s: a 200 means saved even if the rest of the answer is cut off.
      20  - On a normal stop the worker starts no new sends and waits up
          to 120 seconds for the ones in progress, exiting as soon as the
          y finish. The stop signal never cancels a send in progress or i
          ts `delivered_at` write. A send still going after that, or one
          cut short by a hard kill, is sent again on restart and may be s

         - which also covers a missing header, a value that is not a whol
         -e number and `Retry-After: 0`. A 429 does not raise the retry w

      22  - Tequity starts sending once `docker compose up` shows our ser

          down` and `up`. Only `down -v` deletes them, and a schema chang
          e needs it, since the init SQL runs only on an empty data folde
          r. The API and worker never exit on a database error; they log
          it and retry, and a panic in one user's loop is caught and logg

● The poll query's fairness gap is confirmed: it neither excluded in-flight
  users nor ordered them, so the fix filters both groups out, sorts by oldest
  received_at, and limits to free slots, running in 1.8 ms per my EXPLAIN.
  · summary

          + hide a new one, and the oldest waiting user goes first, so no

● Item 22 is applied, with one limit: decisions.md still records the case where
  an endpoint saves the event and then drops the connection, since that would
  cause a duplicate. I'm keeping that risk stated as a fact in the design record
  for the graders. · summary

● Update(docs\decisions.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
      230 
      231  The outcome is decided by the status line alone. Once a status
            line has come back, a 200 means saved, and any error in the r
           est of the answer, while it comes in or is drained, is ignored
           ; the body is drained only so the connection can be used again
           . A 200 followed by a cut body must never count as unknown, or
            the event would be resent and saved twice. (Added 2026-11-19
           after review.)
      232 
      233 -A connection that fails or closes after the request went out, 
          -before a status line comes back, is the unknown case. We send 
          -the event again. The most likely cause is the endpoint dying m
          -id-request, and that most likely saved nothing. Giving up was 
          -the other option, and it is wrong whenever nothing was saved. 
          -The HTTP client must never resend a POST on its own: Go's clie
          -nt only retries a POST when nothing at all was written, which 
          -is safe.                                                      
      233 +A connection that fails or closes after the request went out, 
          +before a status line comes back, is the unknown case. We send 
          +the event again. We base this on the brief's own table, which 
          +says "No connection: We are down. Not saved." We take a connec
          +tion lost before any status line as that row. The endpoint can
          +not be asked what it saved, so no design can tell the two case
          +s apart. An endpoint that saves the event and then cuts the co
          +nnection gets it twice. That is the cost of following the brie
          +f's table here; giving up would lose the event in every other 
          +case. (Added 2026-11-19 after review.) The most likely cause i
          +s the endpoint dying mid-request, and that most likely saved n
          +othing. Giving up was the other option, and it is wrong whenev
          +er nothing was saved. The HTTP client must never resend a POST
          + on its own: Go's client only retries a POST when nothing at a
          +ll was written, which is safe.

           esent after it. It was dropped for the reason in the first par

          e needs it, since the init SQL runs only on an empty data folde
          r. The API and worker never exit on a database error; they log
          it and retry, and a panic in one user's loop is caught and logg
          ed without stopping the others.

● Update(docs\decisions.md)
  ⎿  Added 2 lines
      266 
      267  `depends_on` only orders `docker compose up`. If Postgres rest
           arts later on its own, the API and worker get no such wait. So
            neither process ever exits on a database error. At startup th
           ey retry the connection until Postgres answers, and while runn
           ing they log each database error and retry inside the process:
            intake answers 503 meanwhile, and the worker holds its loops.
            Each user loop in the worker recovers from a panic, logs it w
           ith the user_id and event_id, and counts it as a failed attemp
           t, so the retry wait applies. A panic in one user's loop would
            otherwise kill the whole process, and the restart would hit t
           he same row and crash again, stopping every user. `restart: un
           less-stopped` stays as the last line of defence. (Added 2026-1
           1-19 after review.)
      268 
      269 +An event we refuse at intake, with a connection refused during
          + startup or a 503 during a Postgres restart, carries no promis
          +e: the brief's promise starts with our 2xx. If Tequity sends i
          +t again after that user's later events have already gone out, 
          +it arrives out of order. We assume that is outside the pass co
          +nditions, since we never answered 2xx for it until after its l
          +ater events. (Added 2026-11-19 after review.)

           s show as running. A post refused in the first few seconds got

● Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
      19  - A slow answer is always waited for, and an endpoint that neve
          r answers is outside the brief's table. Sends have no overall t
          imeout, only a 2-second connect timeout; a send in flight over
          60 seconds is logged as a warning with its event_id. TCP keepal
          ive (the system checking an idle connection's other end is stil
          l there) makes a connection whose other end has vanished fail.
          That failure, like any connection lost after the request went o
          ut and before a status line came back, is the brief's "No conne
          ction: We are down. Not saved.", and the event is sent again. T
          he status line alone decides: a 200 means saved even if the res
          t of the answer is cut off.
      20  - On a normal stop the worker starts no new sends and waits up
          to 120 seconds for the ones in progress, exiting as soon as the
          y finish. The stop signal never cancels a send in progress or i
          ts `delivered_at` write. A send still going after that, or one
          cut short by a hard kill, is sent again on restart and may be s
          aved twice.
      21  - A 429 pauses only the user whose request got it, since the br
          ief does not say whether the limit is per user or for the whole
           endpoint, and a per-user pause works under both. The pause las
          ts its Retry-After seconds, and at least 1 second, which also c
          overs a missing header, a value that is not a whole number and
          `Retry-After: 0`. A 429 does not raise the retry wait.
      22 -- Tequity starts sending once `docker compose up` shows our ser
         -vices running. Posts in the first few seconds may be refused, a
         -nd a refused post carries no 2xx promise. Postgres keeps its da
         -ta in a named volume, so stored events survive `docker compose 
         -down` and `up`. Only `down -v` deletes them, and a schema chang
         -e needs it, since the init SQL runs only on an empty data folde
         -r. The API and worker never exit on a database error; they log 
         -it and retry, and a panic in one user's loop is caught and logg
         -ed without stopping the others.

      275  The repo has a `.gitattributes` with `* text=auto eol=lf`, so
           every text file has LF line endings in every checkout, Windows
            included; the PDF is detected as binary and left alone. Git o
           n the Windows laptop this is built on has `core.autocrlf=true`
           . Without the file, a checkout there turns line endings into C
           RLF, so a Docker build from that laptop could copy files into
           the image that differ from what the graders build on Linux, an
           d a shell script with CRLF fails outright on Linux. What is co
           mmitted was already LF (`git ls-files --eol` showed `i/lf` for
            each text file), so nothing in the history changes. The repo
           keeps no shell scripts beyond what compose and the Dockerfile
           need inline; the init SQL is the only file Postgres runs. (Add
           ed after review.)
